### [Dying with Whimsy](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3uRGPDrucg9RLLcp5/dying-with-whimsy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>To me it feels pretty emotionally clear we are nearing the end-times with AI. That in 1-4 years</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8py31p28rdl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8py31p28rdl">[1]</a></sup></span><span> things will be radically transformed, that at least one of the big AI labs will become autonomous research organizations working on developing the next version of their AI, perhaps with some narrow guidance of humans in oversight or acquisition of more resources until robotics is solved too.</span></p><p><span>And i believe there will be some nice benefits at first with this, with the AI organizations providing many goods and services in exchange for money, to raise capital so that the self-improvement resource acquisition loop can continue.</span></p><p><span>But I’m not sure how it will ultimately turn out. Declaring risk of extinction-level events less than 10% seems overconfident. Yet, declaring the risks to be &gt;90% also seems overconfident. But I generally remain quite uncertain about which factors will dominate. Maybe AIs will remain friendly and, for decision theory reasons, continue put some fraction of resources to look after us to some extent, as a signal that future entities should do the same for them. Maybe the loop of capital acquisition is so brutal and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/moloch" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>molochian</span></a><span> that doom wins at least once. And people have been confidently wrong about doom in the past. So i remain unsure. I just say I'm 50:50 on it.</span></p><p><span>But it also feels like, as an individual who does not have any particularly position of influence or power, things are mostly out of my control. There are actions i can take that can maybe push things one way or another. I should seriously do these actions, and the bottleneck feels mostly like not exploring the option space enough.</span></p><p><span>But how should one feel about it all?</span></p><p><span>If one seriously believes that one has ~2 years left where either you die, or actions will become insignificant, what should one do?</span></p><hr /><p><span>One emotion one can feel at first is often a sense of doom and despair. That there is “nothing one can do”. That one should wallow in self-pity, “woe be me, i wish i could have a longer life”. I get it.</span></p><p><span>But also, just get over it.</span></p><p><span>Maybe i find it easier since I have emotionally grappled with conclusions of Nihilism a lot before. But really the only way out is to not care that you will eventually die (whether soon or at the heat-death of the universe), and to try live a good life anyway.</span></p><p><span>But it is possible to just choose a better reaction and not worry about it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref104n7gxqlmnn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn104n7gxqlmnn">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Another emotion one can feel is a frantic “I must do something, I must do something”. I think this is a pretty reasonable emotion to feel. You should probably follow it. It mostly feels like the phases for this are something like: 1) Thinking of one’s own ideas for a while and feeling you can do things. 2) Realizing most of the ideas you thought of are already being tried by others, and feeling hopeless. 3) Realizing that despite this, there is work to be done that could plausibly be useful, and maybe it feels marginal, but you should do it anyway.</span></p><p><span>If doing research, I think it is worth having some loops of [exploring which thing seems actually most useful to do right now] and [spending time exploiting that thing to the point you’ve made some substantial progress]. These days with Claude Code, the latter seems particularly easy to do, and will probably keep getting easier to do. Sometimes this may mean means that the value may be tilted towards [slightly improving the kind of work AI will do in the future] rather than [make something directly useful now]. I guess I think both kinds of work seem valuable, but it’s worth having this in your mind explicitly.</span></p><p><span>There is little enough time that you should do any work you can, but enough time that you need to be considerate on how you spend your time, and not get burnt out</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpdiqbxav9t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpdiqbxav9t">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. If you are the type of person who can spend 100% of their days working on something with deep focus for years, go do that, but you’re probably not reading this if you are.</span></p><hr /><p><span>But I think with these short timelines, there is another thing you can feel, which is that the life you have left may be pretty short. Maybe you will live, maybe not, but even if we live, your life will be so different and transformed.</span></p><p><span>I used to be very emotionally bought-into some things: </span><i><span>delayed-gratification. FIRE. marshmallow test. Being Stoic. Don’t burn bridges. don’t make anyone dislike you. don’t stand out for the wrong reasons. alter your thoughts and behaviors as to not be too cringe. Delay things until after the singularity.</span></i></p><p><span>I think even in normal circumstances, erring too much towards these is not great.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefetztk29oap"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnetztk29oap">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>But with short timelines, it feels like an </span><i><span>extreme</span></i><span> waste of what little valuable time we have left to be exclusively worrying about these things</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7459s6wnw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7459s6wnw">[5]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>You should have fun.</span></p><p><span>You should do things that you think are weird.</span></p><p><span>You should spend your money on things that will improve your life. Yes even that too. I know it’s painful.</span></p><p><span>You should notice the subtle things that make you sad, and not just brush them off, but fix them.</span></p><p><span>Don’t compromise on your morals or other things that don’t need to be compromised.</span></p><p><span>You should get past the awkward roadblock in your head and do that thing.</span></p><p><span>You should get someone to hold you to account for doing the things you really want to do.</span></p><p><span>Go on that trip you want to go on.</span></p><p><span>Be cringe.</span></p><p><span>Sing that karaoke. Do those dance moves. Write that blog post.</span></p><p><span>Ignore the people who might think you are cringe. They don’t really care that much.</span></p><p><span>Put on the cat ears you always wanted to wear.</span></p><p><span>And you will maybe befriend the people who are cringe in just the same ways as you.</span></p><p><span>Life may be very short. So make the next few years the best ones.</span></p><p><span>Live your life with whimsy.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8py31p28rdl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8py31p28rdl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I tend to err to much towards low confidence, but I would say this timeline something like 50% confidence interval. If i think about it, I could see it taking like ~10 years longer, depending on what threshold you want to use for more like 90% confidence conditioned on no AI Pause/moratorium. Emotionally the 1-4 year period feels most correct.</span></p><p><span>I don’t provide evidence for timelines here, I may describe what feels salient to me some at some another time, but other people have put much more effort into describing short timelines.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn104n7gxqlmnn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref104n7gxqlmnn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>yeah skill issue ngl</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpdiqbxav9t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpdiqbxav9t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think noticing you are burnt out can be quite difficult if you’re not sure what it’s like. I felt real guilt at the possibility i could be burnt out, because of guilt on how many of my hours per week felt like i was doing work. I you are even holding the hypothesis, you should probably spend some time seriously considering it. It’s not that bad if you need to spend some time on a real actual break from what you are doing. It might not always feel as pressing from a distance. Other people are doing their own work too.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnetztk29oap"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefetztk29oap">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>law of equal and opposite of advice applies: </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/03/24/should-you-reverse-any-advice-you-hear/"><span>https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/03/24/should-you-reverse-any-advice-you-hear/</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7459s6wnw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7459s6wnw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Exceptions for “I work in a field such as politics/law where reputation with normal people is extremely important”. If you’re not sure and just want to keep option value open, then this exception probably doesn't apply to you. And you still might be erring too much to reputation.</span></p><p><span>Additionally, think things might still turn out fine, so don’t do things that are reckless and put your life at risk in the short term. Avoid physically dangerous activities. Get your cryonics plan sorted. etc.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3uRGPDrucg9RLLcp5/dying-with-whimsy#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Anthropic Responsible Scaling Policy v3: A Matter of Trust](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AkzauoTt2Lwn2yAvj/anthropic-responsible-scaling-policy-v3-a-matter-of-trust)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Anthropic has revised its Responsible Scaling Policy to v3.</p>
<p>The changes involved include abandoning many previous commitments, including one not to move ahead if doing so would be dangerous, citing that given competition they feel blindly following such a principle would not make the world safer.</p>
<p>Holden Karnofsky advocated for the changes. He maintains that the previous strategy of specific commitments was in error, and instead endorses the new strategy of having aspirational goals. He was not at Anthropic when the commitments were made.</p>
<p>My response to this will be two parts.</p>
<p>Today’s post talks about considerations around Anthropic going back on its previous commitments, including asking to what extent Anthropic broke promises or benefited from people reacting to those promises, and how we should respond.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25190"></span>


</div>
<p>It is good, given that Anthropic was not going to keep its promises, that it came out and told us that this was the case, in advance. Thank you for that.</p>
<p>I still think that Anthropic importantly broke promises, that people relied upon, and did so in ways that made future trust and coordination, both with Anthropic and between labs and governments, harder. Admitting to the situation is absolutely the right thing, but doing so does not mean you don’t face the consequences.</p>
<p>Friday’s post dives into the new RSP v3.0 and the accompanying Roadmap and Risk Report, in detail.</p>
<p>Note that yes this is being posted on April Fools Day, but this post is only an April Fools joke insofar as those who believed Anthropic’s previous RSPs are now the April Fool.</p>


<h4>Promises, Promises</h4>


<p>If your initial promises were a mistake, it may or may not be another mistake to walk them back. Either way, even if your promises were not hard commitments, walking them back involves paying a price for having broken your promises, even if you had a strong reason to break them. How big a price depends on the circumstances.</p>
<p>Almost all mainstream coverage of this event framed it as abandoning or walking back Anthropic’s core safety promises, especially ‘do not scale models to a dangerous level without adequate safeguards.’ As a central example of this, <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/anthropic-dials-back-ai-safety-commitments-38257540?mod=e2tw">The Wall Street Journal said ‘Anthropic Dials Back AI Safety Commitments</a>’ due to competitive pressures. That oversimplifies the situation, leaving a lot out, but doesn’t seem wrong.</p>
<p>Many outsiders who follow the situation more closely believe this amounts to Anthropic having broken its commitments. Some go so far as to say this means that lab commitments to safety should not be considered worth the paper that they were never printed on. Many now expect Anthropic to make some amount of effort, but nothing that would much interfere with business plans. If Anthropic can’t make the commitment, why should anyone else? Certainly this government is not going to help.</p>
<p>Don’t be afraid to tell them how you really feel.<a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026788743823372337"> They welcome it.</a> So here we go.</p>


<h4>Anthropic Responsible Scaling Policy v3</h4>


<p>The Responsible Scaling Policy is Anthropic’s commitments regarding when and under what conditions they will release frontier models.</p>
<p>The headline change is that they are no longer committed to not releasing potentially unsafe models, if someone else did it first. Cause, you know, they started it.</p>


<h4>That Could Have Gone Better</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/responsible-scaling-policy-v3">Anthropic starts their new analysis by going over their theory of change from having an RSP at all</a>, and whether those theories were realized. They report a mixed bag.</p>
<p>First, the good news.</p>
<ol>
<li>They developed (modestly) stronger safeguards.</li>
<li>They did successfully implement ASL-3 safeguards.</li>
<li>They did importantly get OpenAI and DeepMind to develop frameworks, and then had the idea of a framework codified in SB 53 and RAISE.</li>
</ol>
<p>Then the bad news.</p>
<ol>
<li>It did not create consensus about the level of risk from various models. It has proven very unclear how much risk is in the room, especially in biology.</li>
<li>Government action has been nonzero but painfully slow at best.</li>
<li>(I would add) We’re not being sufficiently proactive about ASL-4.</li>
<li>(I would add) The requirements got changed somewhat when inconvenient.</li>
</ol>


<h4>I’m Just Not Ready To Make a Commitment</h4>


<p>What’s the most important differences in the new version?</p>
<p>Anthropic is now basically giving up on hard commitments and barriers to releasing models, relying instead on ‘we will make reasonable-to-us arguments’ and decide that the benefits exceed the risks.</p>
<p>I appreciate the honesty. Really, I do.</p>
<p>If you’re not ready to make a commitment, and you realize you shouldn’t have made one, then the second best time to realize and admit that fact is right now.</p>
<p>Officially breaking the commitments now is higher integrity than silently breaking them later. It’s especially better than silently changing the RSP right before a release. I approve of Charles’s frame of ‘<a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026718800977244294">Anthropic stopped pretending to have red lines at which they will unilaterally pause</a>.’</p>
<p>If Anthropic was in practice already doing a ‘we think our arguments are reasonable’ decision process, which with Opus 4.6 it seemed like they mostly were, then better to admit it than to pretend otherwise.</p>
<p>I want to emphasize that essentially no one, not even those who disagree with me and think Anthropic should pause, and who also think Anthropic made rather strong commitments it is now breaking, are saying ‘Anthropic should be holding to its previous commitments purely because they said so, even if this leads to pausing that does not make sense.’</p>
<p>One still has to be held to account for breaking promises, and for making promises that were inevitably going to be broken, even if the decision to break them is right. Your defense that the move was correct does not excuse you from its consequences.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/1a3orn/status/2027073497550499892">1a3orn</a>: Arguments against the Anthropic RSP changes seem to incline towards deontological language regarding broken promises / duties<br />
While arguments for them incline toward consequentialist language / greater good, afaict.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2027077892619080041">Oliver Habryka</a>: I think both are right! The old RSP was obviously unworkable and should have never been published, given what Anthropic is trying to do. So abandoning it is the right thing to do, but of course if you break promises you should be held accountable.</p>
<p>It’s not that hard to explain the consequentialist arguments for holding people accountable for breaking promises, but most people have an intuitive sense for why it’s important, so you don’t have to unpack it.</p>
<p>(To be clear, I think Anthropic should stop scaling and redirect its efforts towards advocating for a pause, but doing that because of the RSP would be weird and I don’t think the right move.</p>
<p>It would just look like you sabotaged yourself and now want to hold others back because you accidentally promised some dumb things that took you out of the race)</p></blockquote>
<p>I also want to emphasize that commitments are only one way to improve safety. Even when plans are worthless, planning is essential, and you can and should just do things. None of this means ‘Holden or Anthropic don’t care about safety,’ only that they will decide what they think is right and then do it, and you can decide how much you trust them to choose wisely.</p>
<p>I do still see this as Anthropic abandoning its experiment on importantly engaging in voluntary self-government and restricting itself. Technically they reserved the right to do it, but it’s still quite the gut punch to do it.</p>
<p>The experiment is over. That’s better than pretending the experiment is working.</p>
<p>From this point, there are no commitments, only statements of intent. Anthropic’s going to do what it’s going to do. You can either choose to trust Anthropic’s leadership to make good decisions, or you can choose not to.</p>
<p>I think Anthropic’s description of its own history says that having these softly binding commitments, and having a track record of treating it as costly to break them, was very good for safety outcomes and policy adoption. I hate that we’ve given that up.</p>


<h4>So Cold, So Alone</h4>


<p>If your commitment is conditional on the actions of others, you should say that.</p>
<p>They didn’t entirely not say this before, but it was very much phrased as ‘in case of emergency we might have to break glass’ rather than ‘we only hold back if everyone relevant signs on.’</p>
<p>RSPv2 said this in 7.1.7: “If another frontier AI developer passes or is about to pass a Capability Threshold without implementing equivalent Required Safeguards, such that their actions pose a serious risk to the world, then because the incremental risk from Anthropic would be small, Anthropic might lower its Required Safeguards. If it did so, it would acknowledge the overall level of risk posed by AI systems (including its own) and invest significantly in making a case to the U.S. government for regulatory action.”</p>
<p>Whereas Anthropic is now saying they’re willing to hit those thresholds first, unless they have explicit commitments from others to do otherwise, even if this is not a small incremental risk.</p>
<p>I strongly <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=j9oXLPjxousMxjwjj">agree with aysja</a>, and disagree with Holden, that it would be misleading to describe this shift as a ‘natural extension of the RSP being a living document.’</p>
<p>I do see the argument that goes like this:</p>
<ol>
<li>Going first was designed to get others to follow in a coordination problem.</li>
<li>No one followed.</li>
<li>That didn’t work, so we should admit it didn’t work and move on.</li>
</ol>
<p>If that is where we are at now, you have all the reason to make this stricter requirement clear up front. That gives others more reason to follow you, and avoids all the nasty headlines we’re seeing now. Alas. it’s a little late for that.</p>
<p>If the mistake has already been made, it’s not obviously bad to admit defeat, and say you’re not going to then let someone else potentially dumber and riskier get there first.</p>
<p>I definitely agree it’s better to announce your intention to violate your old policy now, rather than wait until the day you do violate the old policy, which might never come.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2026721001149522325">davidad</a>: Voluntary commitments to AI slowdowns were a nice idea in 2024 when it was plausible that they could be baby steps toward a multilateral agreement that would contain the intelligence explosion. For a variety of reasons this is no longer plausible.</p>
<p>Anthropic is doing good here.</p>
<p>In the strategic landscape of 2026, racing is the right move, not just for profit but also for maximizing the probability that things go well for most current humans.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sleepinyourhat/status/2026797148566859836">Sam Bowman</a> (Anthropic): I endorse the top [paragraph above].</p>
<p>The Anthropic RSP changes are an attempt to work out what kinds of firm commitments have the most leverage in an environment that’s less promising than we’d expected for policy and coordination.</p>
<p>We misjudged what the environment would look like at this point, which is sad. But these new commitments do still have some heft, including a lot more verifiable transparency (with third parties in the loop) on risks and mitigations.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026804569339277698">Oliver Habryka</a>: I am in favor of figuring out what kind of firm commitments have the most leverage. But of course, you can’t do that by making “firm commitments” directly!</p>
<p>It’s not a firm commitment if you are just playing around with different commitments.</p></blockquote>
<p>The main catch is, it sounds like ‘you should see one of the other guys’ is going to be used as a basically universal excuse to go forward essentially no matter how risky it is, if the cost of not doing so is high?</p>
<p>If Anthropic does in the future pause for an extended period, in a way that is importantly costly, then I will have been wrong about this and precommit to saying so in public. If I don’t do so, please remind me of this.</p>
<p>As Drake Thomas notes, the virtue ethical case for ‘<a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2026777170765558073">don’t impose material existential risk on the planet</a>’ is reasonably strong.</p>
<p>One problem is that this absolutely is going to weaken the willingness of others to incur costs, and embolden those who want to move forward no matter what. Endorsing race logic and the impossibility of cooperation has its consequences.</p>


<h4>I’m Sorry I Gave You That Impression</h4>


<p>What do you mean the RSP was committing Anthropic to things?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/rgblong/status/2026747758791868491">Robert Long</a>: I’m not super read up on RSPs and haven’t read Holden’s post. But it feels similar to the “Anthropic won’t push the capability frontier” meme: not strictly entailed by Anthropic’s official stance, but a strong impression they gave off and benefited from.</p>
<p>is that fair? incomplete?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026748476294574550">Oliver Habryka</a>: I mean, in this case the impression was really extremely unambiguous and strong. I agree the evidence for the promises made in the capability frontier case is largely private and so is externally ambiguous, but in this case we have great receipts!</p>
<p>Here, for example, is a conversation with Evan Hubinger. The conversation starts with someone saying:</p>
<p>Someone: One reason I’m critical of the Anthropic RSP is that it does not make it clear under what conditions it would actually pause, or for how long, or under what safeguards it would determine it’s OK to keep going.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Np5Q3Mhz2AiPtejGN/we-re-not-ready-thoughts-on-pausing-and-responsible-scaling-4?commentId=yEJLXe5PFLomDgziw">Evan Hubinger responded with</a> (across a few different comments): It’s hard to take anything else you’re saying seriously when you say things like this; it seems clear that you just haven’t read Anthropic’s RSP.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>The conditions under which Anthropic commits to pausing in the RSP are very clear. In big bold font on the second page it says:</p>
<p>Anthropic’s commitment to follow the ASL scheme thus implies that we commit to pause the scaling and/or delay the deployment of new models whenever our scaling ability outstrips our ability to comply with the safety procedures for the corresponding ASL.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>the security conditions could trigger a pause all on their own, and there is a commitment to develop conditions that will halt scaling after ASL-3 by the time ASL-3 is reached.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>This is the basic substance of the RSP: I don’t understand how you could have possibly read it and missed this. I don’t want to be mean, but I am really disappointed in these sort of exceedingly lazy takes.</p>
<p>Oliver Habryka: This was, in my experience, routine. I therefore do see this switch from “RSP as concrete if-then-commitments” to “RSP as positive milestone setting” to constitute a meaningful breaking of a promise. Yes, the RSP always said in its exact words that Anthropic could revise it, but people who said that condition would trigger were frequently dismissed and insulted as in the comment above.</p></blockquote>
<p>This certainly sounds like Evan Hubinger basically attacking anyone for daring to question that the RSP represented de facto strong commitments by Anthropic. We now know it did not strongly commit Anthropic to anything.</p>
<p>Evan predicted there was a substantial change Anthropic’s commitments would at some point force it to pause.<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Np5Q3Mhz2AiPtejGN/we-re-not-ready-thoughts-on-pausing-and-responsible-scaling-4?commentId=x3oyQxJxbfEpG7iKt"> Oliver made a market on that</a>, which is now at ~0% despite rapid capabilities progress and Anthropic now arguably being in the lead.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=YYzFLKj2p9DK7dPaF">Even after the RSPv3 release, Evan Hubinger continued to defend his position</a>, that he was only saying that the RSP made a clear statement about where the lines were, not that the lines would not change or actually work in practice. Like Oliver I find this highly convincing given a plain reading of Evan’s comment. I do appreciate Evan saying now that we should downweight the theory of RSPs.</p>
<p>So the question then becomes, were Evan Hubinger and other employees who talked similarly under a false impression? If so, why? If not, why talk this way?</p>
<p>Oliver Habryka could not be more clear here, and I don’t think he would lie about this:</p>
<blockquote><p>Oliver <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=zEt5fkFDeWBrQn4J8">Habryka</a>: Yes, Anthropic employees on more than a dozen occasions told me that the RSP binds them to a mast. I had many very explicit conversations with many Anthropic employees about this, because I was following up on what <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PBd7xPAh22y66rbme/anthropic-s-leading-researchers-acted-as-moderate?commentId=eKSdqu8codZfGEN6p">I thought was Anthropic violating what I perceived to be a promise to not push forward the state of AI capabilities</a>, which many employees disputed had happened.</p>
<p>… At various events I was at, and conversations I had with people, Anthropic employees told me they were aiming to achieve robustness from state-backed hacking programs, and that they were ready to pause if they could not achieve that (as the RSP “committed” them to such things).</p></blockquote>
<p>Oliver notes that Holden Karnofsky in particular has previously communicated he felt this was a different and lower level of commitment, that is consistent with him pushing the changes in v3, in contrast to many other Anthropic employees.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026718800977244294">As Oliver Habryka says here</a>, if Evan was under this false impression, Anthropic benefited enormously from giving its senior employees like Evan this impression. This does not seem like a ‘mistake’ from Anthropic to do this, and it would not be reasonable from the outside view to consider it an accident.</p>
<p>At minimum, if you don’t admit Anthropic has importantly now broken its commitments, then this is all highly misleading use of the word ‘commitment.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026750662785249386">Oliver Habryka</a>: I would be pretty surprised if the employees in-question here end up saying they were deceived. Also, these are high-level enough employees that it’s unclear what it even means for them to be “deceived”. Deceived by whom? They drafted the RSP! They almost certainly were also involved in the decision to change it.</p>
<p>They benefitted hugely from this by getting social license to work at Anthropic and having people get off their back, and they are now at least deca-millionaires (or often billionaires).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rgblong/status/2026760214419222819">Robert Long</a>: fwiw I take that disagreement to be semantic, about “commitment” (as you note). I also agree with what you said then about the connotation of “commitment” – s.t. calling RSPs commitments means he should’ve fought the change and/or now own “we decided to break our commitment”</p></blockquote>
<p>In particular, yes, <a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2030424224435974247">a lot of people who care about not dying felt that the central point of RSPs was as a de facto compromise</a>, an attempt to put an <a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2024/09/if-then-commitments-for-ai-risk-reduction">if-then commitment trigger</a> on slowing down or pausing. If you couldn’t match the conditions, then you have to pause, which makes it acceptable to move forward now.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=DYvgbmrJiDNd7iLMb">Indeed one could go further</a>. The entire program of focusing heavily on not only Anthropic but evaluation-based organizations like METR and Apollo was that the evals could constitute the if that triggers a then. We now know that such commitments do not work, and that when models pass the dangerous capability tests even Anthropic will likely then fall back upon vibes. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=WriWrtXL6oGqdchF6">METR’s theory of change is</a> ‘ensure the world is not surprised’ but I expect them to still be surprised.</p>
<p>Alternatively or in addition, you can interpret it as Holden does, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=nE5vgoxNfJwbY9zf2">that ‘no one has any willingness to slow down, and until there is a crisis this won’t change</a>.’ Now the attitude is essentially ‘pausing or slowing down would be akin to suicide for a frontier AI lab, so things would have to be super extreme to do that, this is more of a plan we aspire to.’ Which is also a fine thing, but a very different style of document. Those who thought it was the first type of document lose Bayes points. Whereas those who thought it was the second type of document win Bayes points.</p>
<p>One could interpret a lot of this as ‘Anthropic employees implied they were using Rationalist epistemic norms, but instead they were using a different set of norms.’</p>


<h4>Fool Me Twice</h4>


<p>Does this backtrack remind you of anything?</p>
<p>It should. In particular, it should remind you of what happened with the idea that Anthropic would not ‘push the frontier of AI capabilities.’</p>
<p>A lot of people told us, with various wordings and degrees of commitment attached, that Anthropic would not do that. Then Anthropic sort of did it. Then they totally flat out did it and now Claude Code and Claude Opus 4.6 are very clearly the frontier.</p>
<p>Then we were told, ‘oh we never promised not to do that.’</p>
<p>Maybe they didn’t strictly promise to do that. Maybe a lot of telephone games were involved, but Anthropic at minimum damn well should have known that a lot of people were under that impression. I was under that impression. And they knew that people were making major life decisions, and deciding whether and how much to support Anthropic, on the basis of that decision, with no sign anyone ever did anything to correct the record.</p>
<p>Now we’re being told, again, ‘oh we never promised not to [undo our commitments].</p>
<p>You’re trying to tell us what about your new commitments, then?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ruben_bloom/status/2026843166696235346">Ruben Bloom (Ruby)</a>: I don’t like the pattern. In 2022, I was told that “Anthropic commits to not push the frontier” as reason to worry less. Later that was abandoned and the story for Anthropic’s safety was the RSP. That too has caved.</p>
<p>By “I was told”, I mean the specific things said to me in conversation with Anthropic employees who were justifying their participation in a company participating in the AI race.</p>
<p>It’s just such a bitter “I told you so”, when you predicted years that ago competitive pressures would erode any and perhaps all commitments.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2026855944458735651">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: If I’d ever had the faintest, tiniest credence in Anthropic’s “Responsible Scaling Policy”, I’d probably feel pretty betrayed right now!</p>
<p>As it is, I ask only that you update, and not always be surprised in the same direction of “huh, Eliezer was right to call it empty”.</p>
<p>Note: to observe how my cynicism repeatedly *ends up* right, tally only how things *end up*. Don’t jump and say “See, Eliezer was wrong to be cynical!” the moment you hear an uncashed promise or see an arguable sign of later hope.</p></blockquote>
<p>Eliezer and others are constantly getting flak for predicting things that, in broad terms, do indeed seem to keep on reliably happening, everywhere. People constantly say ‘we will not do [X]’ or ‘in that case we would definitely do [Y]’ or heaven forbid ‘no one would be so stupid as to [Z]’ and then you turn around those same people did [X] and didn’t do [Y] and a lot of people did [Z] and you’re treated as a naive idiot for having ever taken the alternative seriously.</p>
<p>Best update your priors. All the people who said commitments wouldn’t hold get Bayes points. Those who didn’t lose Bayes points.</p>
<p>All the people who are now saying new ‘commitments’ matter and they really mean it this time? They don’t matter zero, but they are not true commitment.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ruben_bloom/status/2026844528280506428">I also don’t understand</a>, given its composition and past Anthropic actions, why I should put that much stock in the Long Term Benefit Trust. It’s better to have it in its current form than not have it, but it was an important missed opportunity.</p>
<p>Anthropic definitely gets meaningful points on this front for standing up for what it believed in during the confrontation with the Department of War, even if you think those particular choices were unwise. I think there’s a lot more hope for actions of the form ‘Anthropic or another lab takes this particular stand right now’ than ‘Anthropic or another lab will take this particular stand later.’</p>


<h4>In My Defense I Was Left Unsupervised</h4>


<p><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/DGZNAGL2FNJfftwgE/responsible-scaling-policy-v3-1">Holden offers a defense of the new RSP here</a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=zEt5fkFDeWBrQn4J8"> and here</a>, essentially saying that binding commitments are bad, because we don’t have enough information to choose them wisely, so you might choose poorly and regret them later, and indeed Anthropic did previously sometimes choose poorly and now is later and they’re regretting it. So sayeth all those who wish to not make any binding commitments.</p>
<p>I interpret Holden, despite his saying he has a document where he wrote down where he would think a unilateral pause would be a good idea, as saying that they are going to do their best to do appropriate mitigations, but ultimately yes, they are going to release models, both internally and externally, pretty much no matter what mitigations are or are not available short of ‘okay yeah this is obviously a really terrible idea that will get us all killed or at least blow up directly in our faces,’ and they’re simply admitting this was always true. Okay, then.</p>
<p>Holden basically says in particular that he doesn’t think Anthropic should slow down based on inability to prevent theft of model weights, even if it crosses the ‘AI R&amp;D-5’ threshold that is at least singularity-ish. They’re going to go ahead regardless. They’re not going to stop. I worry a lot both about the not stopping, and that without the forcing function of having to stop, they even more so than before won’t invest sufficiently in the necessary precautions, here or elsewhere. They not only can’t stop, won’t stop, they won’t halt and catch fire.</p>
<p>A list of aspirational goals is a good thing to have. I don’t think a list of aspirational goals is going to create sufficient threat of looking terrible to provide the same incentives here. That doesn’t mean the list of goals cannot do good work in other ways.</p>
<p>I see Holden complaining a lot about people ‘seeing RSPs as having hard commitments’ and using that as an additional reason to get rid of all the commitments. He’s pointing to all the complaining that Anthropic just broke its commitments and saying ‘see? This reaction is all the more reason we had to break all our commitments.’</p>
<p>It was exactly the enforcement mechanism that, if you break the commitments, people will get mad at you. This is why we can’t <s>stay alive</s> have nice things. So now we will have aspirations.</p>
<p>Aspirations are helpful, they substantially raise the chance you will do the thing, but they are weak precommitment devices when you decide you won’t do the thing later.</p>
<p>I also think his own argument of ‘it’s much easier to require things labs already committed to doing’ works directly against the ‘don’t commit to anything’ plan.</p>


<h4>Drake Thomas Finds The Missing Mood</h4>


<p>Drake Thomas thinks the move from v2.2 to v3.0 is an improvement, while noticing the need to have something like mourning or grief for the spirit of the original v1.0, which is now gone and proven not viable in practice at Anthropic.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2026513179690545586">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): (1) In reading drafts of this RSP and orienting to it, I’ve felt something like mourning or grief for the spirit of the original v1.0 RSP. (Quite a lot of the v1 RSP carries over to v3, but here I’m thinking specifically of the vibe of “specify very crisp capability thresholds at which to trigger very concrete safety mitigations, or else halt development”.)</p>
<p>I think this original approach is ultimately just a pretty bad way for responsible AI developers to set safety policies, leads to misprioritization and bad outcomes, has distortionary effects on incentives and epistemics, and doesn’t achieve much risk reduction in the environment of 2026.</p>
<p>… Accountability! The vibe of RSP v1 sort of rested all accountability in this sense of the commitments as this fixed immutable thing Anthropic would have to stand behind Or Else. I think this is good in some ways and under some threat models, but I think then and now there was less feedback than I’d like on the question of “are the things Anthropic is committing to actually good and useful for safety?” In v3, I think external accountability on these questions is now more loadbearing, and there’s more detailed substance to fuel such accountability. Which leads me to…</p>
<p>Feedback! … I expect the discourse to be very undersupplied with takes on the question of “is the actual v3 policy a good one with good consequences”. Personally I think it is, and a substantial improvement over previous RSPs!</p>
<p>Please actually read and criticize it! Gripe about the ambiguity of the roadmaps! Run experiments to cast doubt on risk report methodology! I can name three significant complaints I have with the RSP off the top of my head and I expect to see none of them on X, prove me wrong!</p></blockquote>
<p>I get Drake’s frustrations. But yes, most people are going to litigate the removal of the core commitment around pausing and general revelation that so-called commitments aren’t so meaningful after all. Most attention is going to go there. <a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2031217463921402341">He makes clear that he gets it</a>, and I’d say he passes the ITT about why people are and have a right to be pissed off, especially that we had language in v1.0 saying that the bar for altering commitments was a lot higher than it ultimately was.</p>
<p>And indeed, a lot of our attention likely should go there, because if the new statements aren’t commitments, it is a lot harder to productively critique them.</p>


<h4>Things That Could Have Been Brought To My Attention Yesterday (1)</h4>


<p>Well, you see, not rushing ahead as fast as possible might slow us down. That would be bad. You wouldn’t want us to do that, would you?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://time.com/7380854/exclusive-anthropic-drops-flagship-safety-pledge/">Jared Kaplan (Chief Science Officer, Anthropic</a>): ​We felt that it wouldn’t actually help anyone for us to stop training AI models. We didn’t really feel, with the rapid advance of AI, that it made sense for us to make unilateral commitments … if competitors are blazing ahead.</p>
<p>…I don’t think we’re making any kind of U-turn.</p></blockquote>
<p>Besides, we aren’t able to evaluate models as fast as we are able to improve them, which means we should triage the evaluations and kind of wing it. I mean, what do you want us to do, not release frontier AI models we can’t evaluate? Silly wabbit.</p>
<blockquote><p>Chris Painter (METR): Anthropic believes it needs to shift into triage mode with its safety plans, because methods to assess and mitigate risk are not keeping up with the pace of capabilities.​</p>
<p>This is more evidence that society is not prepared for the potential catastrophic risks posed by AI.</p>
<p>I like the emphasis on transparent risk reporting and publicly verifiable safety roadmaps.</p>
<p><a href="https://time.com/7380854/exclusive-anthropic-drops-flagship-safety-pledge/">Billy Perrigo</a>: But he said he was “concerned” that moving away from binary thresholds under the previous RSP, by which the arrival of a certain capability could act as a tripwire to temporarily halt Anthropic’s AI development, might enable a “frog-boiling” effect, where danger slowly ramps up without a single moment that sets off alarms.</p></blockquote>
<p>That does seem likely and sound concerning.</p>


<h4>Things That Could Have Been Brought To My Attention Yesterday (2)</h4>


<p>In other need-to-know news, Sean asked a very good question. Drake’s answer to this was about as good as one could have hoped for, given the facts.</p>
<p>If you’ve decided to break your ‘commitment,’ you want to tell us as soon as possible.</p>
<p>I have confirmation that the board only approved the changes ‘very recently.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2026956416376799393">Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</a>: At what point was it decided that the previous commitment were ‘subject to a favourable environment’ and not ‘firm commitments’, and was this communicated across staff? The whole point of commitments is an expectation of being able to rely on them when the environment is not favourable, not just when they’re easy to make.</p>
<p>It also seems clear at this point that these commitments were presented as harder than this, and used by Anthropic/their staff to<br />
(a)<a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026764952615432341/photo/1"> dismiss and undermine critics<br />
</a> (b)<a href="https://lesswrong.com/posts/MNpBCtmZmqD7yk4q8/my-understanding-of-anthropic-strategy"> in recruitment of safety-concerned talent<br />
</a>(c) in arguing for voluntary if-then commitments at a time when there was more government appetite for considering harder regulation.</p>
<p>I think it’s plausible (though can’t yet confirm) that (d) they’ve also been used in securing investment from safety-conscious investors.</p>
<p>Do you disagree with these claims? If not, do you feel Anthropic has held itself to a standard of ethics and transparency in this (quite important!) matter that is acceptable?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2027179455979167809">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): Re: “at what point was it decided” – I think this presupposes a frame in which this kind of thing is extremely formally pinned down much more than I think it generally is in reality (not just at Anthropic, but in almost all circumstances like this)?</p>
<p>None of the versions of the RSP are particularly clear about exactly what a “commitment” is supposed to be read as, how that should be interpreted within a document which is expected to be amended in the future, what the stakes of violating such a commitment are, etc. Especially the early versions had huge decision-critical ambiguities you could drive a truck through!</p>
<p>It’s not like there was a secret internal RSP which had even more footnotes about meta-commitments that made this dramatically clearer, just a bundle of authorial intent and something-like-case-law and an understanding of what reasonable decisions to reduce risk would be and long-simmering drafts of less ambiguous updated policies that took ages to ship.</p>
<p>To the extent I think there’s something like an answer to the “at what point” question, I know of early discussion around something like an RSP v3 regime widely accessible to Anthropic staff as early as January 2025 and even wider visibility into drafts of something pretty similar to this RSP for at least the past 3 months, though again I don’t think it’s like there was ever some formal conception that this was Forbidden which had to change at a discrete point.</p>
<p>All that said: I think the vibes of Anthropic and much of the v1.0 text and many of its employees’ statements around the RSP circa 2023 and 2024 presented a much more ironclad view of these commitments than is reflected in RSP v3 (and much more than I now think made sense), and I think this reflected pretty poor judgement and merits criticism. (I count myself among the Anthropic employees who acted poorly in hindsight here, though AFAIK Holden has been consistent and reasonable on this since the beginning.)</p>
<p>I think it has been the case and will continue to be the case that Anthropic is abiding by the things it says it is abiding by in its published policies and commitments (and should be loudly criticized for failures to do so), but I think the track record of “things that EAs believe Anthropic to have committed to in perpetuity no matter what no takesies-backies” looks quite bad and I don’t think it goes well to interpret such claims as meaning anything that strong (nor for Anthropic, or almost anyone, to make such commitments in the vast majority of situations).</p>
<p>Wrt the claims here, my sense is:<br />
(a) Eh, I think the specific (LW comment quoted in another comment screenshotted in a tweet linked by you above) is taken out of context and wasn’t really claiming anything in particular about how to interpret the strength of RSP v1 commitments. I do expect this kind of thing happened but I think habryka’s quote is a bad example of it.<br />
(b) Yeah, I think non-frontier-pushing rhetoric was a significantly bigger deal on this front but RSP stuff definitely played some role. To the extent I bear some responsibility for this sort of thing I regret it, though iirc I have been pretty open around thinking unilateral pauses were relatively unlikely for a while.<br />
(c) Hm, I view the intent and expected-at-the-time-effect of RSP v1 style commitments as increasing the odds of codifying such if-then commitments into regulation, by showing them to work well at companies and getting them closer to an existing industry standard. They ultimately failed at doing so, in part due to changing political will, in part due to somewhat limited substantive uptake at other companies, and in part due to the problem where really precise if-then commitments did *not* work all that well because specifying crisp thresholds years in advance in a sensible way was extremely hard – but I think this latter bit is kind of a success story, in that the point of demoing safety policies as voluntary commitments is that if it turns out to be a bad idea you haven’t locked yourself into silly regulation that ends up net bad for x-risk via backlash. Could you say more about how you see the comms around commitment strength having worsened regulation prospects?<br />
(d) not gonna comment on internal fundraising considerations, but checking that you aren’t thinking of the Series A, which happened well before the RSP was introduced?</p></blockquote>
<p>There is then a discussion of how to think about ‘Oliver is right in general but this particular quote is a bad example,’ which I find to be a helpful thing to say if that’s what you think.</p>


<h4>What We Have Here Is A Failure To Communicate</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3?commentId=fAmbuCmRQ7GNpxADf">I think this is also important context</a>. Dario Amodei and Anthropic have been consistently unwilling, with notably rare exceptions, to say the full situation out loud, or to treat it with proper urgency. Yes, you should see the other guy and all that, fair point, but when you are saying ‘no one wants to [X] so we have to change our plan’ you need to have been calling for [X] and explaining why, and also loudly explaining that this is terrible and forcing you to change plans.</p>
<p>I don’t see that type of communication out of Anthropic leadership, over the course of years.</p>
<blockquote><p>Holden Karnofsky: If there were strong and broad political will for treating AI like nuclear power and slowing it down arbitrarily much to keep risks low, the situation might be different. But that isn’t the world we’re in now, and I fear that “overreaching” can be costly.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3#j9oXLPjxousMxjwjj">I.M.J. McInnis</a>: I think it would make a nontrivial contribution to that  ‘strong and broad political will’ if Dario were to come out and say “actually, sorry about all that deliberate Overton-window-closing I did in previous writings. In fact, political will is not a totally exogenous oh-well thing, but it is the responsibility of frontier developers to inculcate that political will by telling the public that a pause is <em>possible </em>and <em>desirable</em>, instead of a dumb lame thing not even worth considering. So now we’re saying loud and clear: a pause is possible and desirable, and the world should work toward it as a Plan A!”</p>
<p>I’m being deliberately cartoonish here, but you get the point. If incentives are forcing Anthropic to abandon things that are good for human survival––which occurrence was, no offense, <em>completely obvious from day one––</em>Anthropic should be screaming from the rooftops, <em>Help!! Incentives are forcing us to abandon things that are good for human survival!!</em></p>
<p>If this is a crux for you––if you/Anthropic think a pause is so undesirable/unlikely that it’s important for the safety of the human race to publicly disparage the possibility of a pause (as Dario opens many of his essays by doing)––please say so! Otherwise, this lily-livered, disingenuous, “oh no, the incentives! it’s a shame incentives can never be changed!” moping will give us all an undignified death.</p>
<p>To be clear, I’m not actually mad about the weakening of the RSP; that was priced in. I suppose I’m glad it’s stated, in case there were still naïfs who thought A Good Guy With An AI could save us. It’s far more virtuous than outright lying, as every other company (to my knowledge) does (more of).</p>
<p>Also, although you seemed to try to answer “What is the point of making commitments if you can revise them any time?” You really just replied “Well, actually these commitments were inconvenient to revise, and in fact they should be more convenient to revise, albeit not arbitrary convenience.” Forgive me if I am not reassured!</p>
<p>I respect your work a lot, Holden. You’ve done great things for humanity. Please don’t lose the forest for the trees.</p></blockquote>


<h4>You Should See The Other Guy</h4>


<p>But they assure us it’s all fine, they are committed to doing as well or better than rivals.</p>
<blockquote><p>Jared Kaplan: If all of our competitors are transparently doing the right thing when it comes to catastrophic risk, we are committed to doing as well or better.​</p>
<p>But we don’t think it makes sense for us to stop engaging with AI research, AI safety, and most likely lose relevance as an innovator who understands the frontier of the technology, in a scenario where others are going ahead and we’re not actually contributing any additional risk to the ecosystem.</p></blockquote>
<p>So, first off, no. As I discussed above, you’re not committed.<a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026524726412325204"> Stop saying you’re committed to things</a> you’re not committed to. You keep using that word.</p>
<p>We’ve just established you can and will back out of ‘commitments’ if you change your mind. You don’t to say ‘commitment’ in an unqualified way anymore, sorry.</p>
<p>Even if we assume this ‘commitment’ is honored, reality does not grade on a curve. Saying ‘I will be as responsible as the least responsible major rival’ is no comfort. You’re Anthropic. If that’s your standard, then you’re not helping matters.</p>
<p>The good news is I expect Anthropic to still do much better than that standard. But that’s purely because I think and hope they will choose to do better. It’s not because I think they are committed to anything.</p>
<p>I don’t want to hear Anthropic or any of its employees say they are ‘committed’ to something unless they are actually committed to it, ever again.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/CFGeek/status/2026454068030566604">Charles Foster</a>: To my knowledge this is the first time a frontier AI developer has explicitly made such a claim about the gap between its internal and external models.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2026476896477917349">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): And under RSP v3, is committed (for sufficiently more capable or widely-autonomously-deployed models) to doing so in the future! Really stoked to move into a regime where risk reporting looks beyond external deployment as the source of danger.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026524726412325204">Oliver Habryka</a>: Come on, let’s not immediately start using the word “committed” again, just after that went very badly.</p>
<p>The right word at this point seems “and as expressed in the RSP, is intending to do X going forward”.</p>
<p>I also think separately from that, Anthropic has I think tried pretty hard with the 2.2 -&gt; 3 transition to disavow much of any of the usual social aspects of a commitment. Like clearly I can’t go to anyone at Anthropic and be like “you broke a commitment” if they don’t do this. They will definitely tell me “what do you mean, Holden wrote a whole post about how this is definitely not a commitment, you can’t come to me and call it a commitment again now”.</p>
<p>Hence it’s quite clearly not a commitment.</p></blockquote>
<p>Drake counteroffers ‘committed to under this policy’ but no, I think that’s wrong. I think the right word is ‘intending.’</p>


<h4>I Was Only Kidding</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://time.com/7380854/exclusive-anthropic-drops-flagship-safety-pledge/">Billy Perrigo</a>: Anthropic, the wildly successful AI company that has cast itself as the most safety-conscious of the top research labs, is dropping the central pledge of its flagship safety policy, company officials tell TIME.</p>
<p>In 2023, Anthropic committed to never train an AI system unless it could guarantee in advance that the company’s safety measures were adequate.</p>
<p>… In recent months the company decided to radically overhaul the RSP. That decision included scrapping the promise to not release AI models if Anthropic can’t guarantee proper risk mitigations in advance.</p>
<p>… Overall, the change to the RSP leaves Anthropic far less constrained by its own safety policies, which previously categorically barred it from training models above a certain level if appropriate safety measures weren’t already in place.</p></blockquote>


<h4>They Can’t Keep Getting Away With This</h4>


<p>Actually, it kind of seems like they can and probably will.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tegmark/status/2026408922194522324">Max Tegmark</a>: Anthropic 2024: You can trust that we’ll keep all our safety promises<br />
Antropic 2026: Nvm</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2026593543536701879">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: So far as I can currently recall, every single time an AI company promises that they’ll do an expensive safe thing later, they renege as soon as the bill comes due.</p>
<p>One single exception: Demis Hassabis turning down higher offers for Deepmind to go with Google and an ethics board. In this case, of course, Google just fucked him on the ethics board promises; but Demis himself did keep to his way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AISafetyMemes/status/2026618994728333615">AI Notkilleveryoneism Memes</a>: Shocked, shocked</p></blockquote>


<h4>Damn Your Sudden But Inevitable Betrayal</h4>


<p>If the betrayal was inevitable, there are two ways to view that.</p>
<ol>
<li>Move along, nothing to see here.</li>
<li>That’s worse. You know that’s worse, right?</li>
</ol>
<p>It makes the particular incident sting less, but it also means they’ll betray you again, and you should model them as the type of people who do a lot of this betrayal thing.</p>
<p>I mean, when Darth Vader says ‘I am altering the deal, pray I do not alter it any further’ it’s a you problem if you’re changing your opinion of Darth Vader, but also you should expect him to be altering the deal again.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2026405702651228553">Garrison Lovely</a>: Welp, the inevitable ultimate backtracking just happened. Anthropic scrapped “the promise to not release AI models if Anthropic can’t guarantee proper risk mitigations in advance.”</p>
<p>Once you’ve decided the race is better with you in it, you can never decide not to race. Anthropic shouldn’t have made promises that it was extremely foreseeable they would not be able to keep. Our plan cannot be to count on “good guys” to “win” the AI race. This also isn’t their first time.</p>
<p>Anthropic deserves credit for standing up to authoritarianism, especially as others capitulate. But self-regulation is and has always been a farce, and these companies are more alike than different. They will always disappoint you.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2026783790899900436">Rob Bensinger</a>: I notice myself slowly coming around as I observe the dynamics at AI labs. Like, I feel like I might have made better inside-view predictions about Anthropic and OpenAI if I’d done more “naively assume that lots of EA-ish people are similar to SBF and his sphere”:</p>
<p>– prone to rationalizing unethical and harmful behavior, like promise-breaking and deception, based on pretty shallow utilitarian reasoning</p>
<p>– comfortable with crazy, out-of-distribution levels of risk-taking</p>
<p>– willing to impose huge externalities on others, without asking their consent</p>
<p>– fixated on power / influence / status / being in the room where it happens.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2026784503101010283">Oliver Habryka</a>: I am glad you are coming around! I mean, I am sad, of course, that this is the right update to make, but I do think it’s true, and am in favor of you and others thinking about what it implies for the future and what to do.</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay. That all needed to be said. On Friday I’ll look at the new RSP on its own merits.</p>


<h4></h4><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AkzauoTt2Lwn2yAvj/anthropic-responsible-scaling-policy-v3-a-matter-of-trust#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Chat, is this sus?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NhHnm2kw3JfbHD4T8/chat-is-this-sus)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>A large assumption we have made in AI control is that humans will be perfect at </span><i><span>auditing</span></i><span>, that is, being shown a transcript and determining if the AI was scheming in that transcript.</span></p><p><span>But we are uncertain whether humans will be perfect at auditing; they are prone to fatigue and distraction. That is why I’m releasing "Sentinel" today, an extremely high-stimulation way to audit boring transcripts.</span></p><p><span>Sentinel is a revolutionary way to get more juice out of your human auditors by gamifying the auditing process with a level system, perks, power-ups, and more fun features. Try it now </span><a href="https://brain-rot-transcript-auditor.vercel.app/"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>In AI control literature, we love finding the safety/usefulness trade-offs of everything we create, but surprisingly, we noticed </span><i><span>no trade-offs with this product</span></i></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4f812a849b61c8bcf523731f55a547c429fc9b0eb5d61661a2b8edd201e5aa59/odybjx6euj2j6otaijjd" /><p><span>The rest of the post will go over some of the ways we achieved this</span></p><h3><span>Gamification</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/85afe5f1f7a33c375e6230b12356ca656b9b6f489ec8dd35064c68f681d143f5/flhtnxvomdiwtjyetrvm" /><p><span>As you audit the transcript in the game, you gain tokens that you can spend on power-ups that make you even more productive. There are also achievement and level systems, so you can see your progress and get more dopamine hits!</span></p><h3><span>Twitch Streaming Mode</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e4975b57d6dcdf4f5d55be4a15b9ce760ce175114c6ccd33fb7c1ae00f75ffd1/hyqktkvg2z7jkpo1u956" /><p><span>AIs might be able to uplift human auditors in the future, which is why Sentinel ships with a Twitch streamer mode where you can go live on Twitch and have trusted monitors view your transcript and give you live feedback!</span></p><h3><span>Subway Surfers</span></h3><p><span>To maximize “eyes on the screen time”, we also added a Subway Surfers feature that lets you watch endless videos of pros playing the game, so you are always stimulated while looking over evil transcripts.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6ca1af61f2491ca1cc83a95c4f1b2319b1159b44ca629fb40cd1eb13aac5169b/ld9zujksswqfkbamb895" /><p><span>Funny aside: While making this game, I asked Claude to implement this Subway Surfers feature, and instead of finding a YouTube video of someone playing Subway Surfers, it rick-rolled me...</span></p><h3><span>Looking Forward</span></h3><p><span>We hope you like this tool. Go play it now to get ready for the upcoming future where the only way AI safety researchers can have an impact is to audit transcripts!</span></p><p><span>(If it wasn't clear, this is a joke and not a real product. I vibecodded it over a couple of hours)</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NhHnm2kw3JfbHD4T8/chat-is-this-sus#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Sun Shrimp Welfare](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/z9yAnPiS9CoruFXan/sun-shrimp-welfare)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>The supposition that we live in a "goldilocks zone" is frankly just nonsense built up by an anthropocentric need to feel self-important, like Copernicus I am here to rescue us from a  self-absorbed disaster of thought. Indeed, what is required for life to form is the ability to create complex structures with causal persistence times above a threshold. With this in mind we are able to find </span><i><span>many</span></i><span> areas where organisms could persist, if we just had the eyes to see them, namely the Sun!</span></p><p><span>The surface of the Sun is frankly massive, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, in contrast consider the habitable region of earth - excluding oceans below photic zones, deserts, and ice caps - which is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The Suns surface exceeds ours by a factor of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, so is it not possible there is more to it than meets the eye?</span></p><p><span>Following </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37794189/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Sharma</span><i><span> et. al</span></i><span> (2023)</span></a><span> we are able to define the assembly index, a, of an object as the minimum number of joining steps required to construct it from some basic building blocks. Life, broadly, requires objects where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> but does not make any assumption about precisely </span><i><span>what</span></i><span> the base building blocks are. It need not be amino acids, nucleic acids, sugars, carbon, or substances that survive a balmy 5,778 K.</span></p><p><span>In the solar photosphere there is a diverse set of possible chemistries to access through complex ionized species, magnetic flux tubes, and granular convection cells capable of spanning ~1,000 km a piece. A single supergranulation cell can persist for ~24-48 hours, an eternity by the standards of plasma recombination (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). The ratio of structural persistence is thus </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, an order of magnitude larger than the equivalent ratio for biochemistry on Earth (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for protein folding compared to bond vibrations). The only conclusion is that the solar photosphere is, by our measure, </span><i><span>more hospitable to complex structure formation than Earth</span></i><span>.</span></p><h1><span>The Sun Shrimp</span></h1><p><span>In this post I have decided to name these creatures Sun Shrimp (Solis caridea), as this is a more cuddly visual that counterweights our own bias towards Earth organisms. We shall define a Sun Shrimp as a self-replicating magnetohydrodynamic structure that satisfies </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, with a characteristic length scale that is on the order of a granulation cell (~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> km) and reproduction time </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>As each granulation cell turns over on the timescale </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> min and within each cell the relevant degrees of freedom are ionized species, magnetic field line topologies, and convective flow configurations. We can estimate the number of independent micro-assembly events per granulation turnover conservatively as the number of Debye-length volumes in a granulation cell:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Giving a total rate of trials across the solar surface, assuming 1 million cells, of:</span><br /><br /><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>From there we need the probability per trial, we only need one Sun Shrimp to have formed in Sol's 4.6 billion year lifespan (</span><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>The groundbreaking paper by </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10355099" rel="noreferrer"><span>Mizuuchi et al. (2023)</span></a><span> demonstrated a 20-nucleotide (nt) RNA oligomer that can self-reproduce via template-directed ligation. The naive probability of that occurring is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, but </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63151-5" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lambert </span><i><span>et al.</span></i><span> (2025)</span></a><span> determined that there are approximately </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> possible autocatalytic RNA sequences. A massive degeneracy in the space of viable self-replicators. As such, even the most pessimistic of estimates requiring specific sequences, our </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of  </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is </span><b><span>32 orders of magnitude more generous</span></b><span> than earth-based abiogenesis achieved with four nucleotide bases, room temperature, and no magnetic confinement.</span></p><p><span>If you accept that life arose on Earth (which since you are reading this I assume you do), then rejecting the possibility of Sun Shrimp requires you believe that a magnetohydrodynamic self-assembly is </span><i><span>harder</span></i><span> than biochemistry. A stance that can only be described as carbon-chauvinism.</span></p><h3><span>Sun Shrimp Reproduction</span></h3><p><span>We can estimate a lower bound on \tau_r from the Alfvén crossing time of a granulation cell:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Even given a pessimistic reproduction time of an hour, this would mean a Sun Shrimp could tile the entire solar surface in:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>One day.</span></p><p><span>A single Sun Shrimp could, left to its own devices, tile the surface of the Sun in a single day. But we do not see a uniform monoculture on the surface of the Sun, which tells us something profound, </span><i><span>they have predators</span></i><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Shrimp Complexity</span></h2><p><span>We have established that Sun Shrimp are likely to have formed, and that their reproduction rate implies a rich ecology. But how </span><i><span>complex</span></i><span> could a Sun Shrimp be?</span></p><p><span>The minimum unit of collective behavior in a plasma is the Debye sphere, a volume of radius </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> where electromagnetic interactions are coherent and information can be stored and transmitted. For us, this is the plasma equivalent of a neuron. In the solar photosphere, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. A Sun Shrimp, spanning one granulation cell (~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), therefore contains a maximum of:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>Now consider every neuron on Earth. Humanity contributes ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons (8 billion brains × 86 billion neurons each). But we are not the main event. Earth hosts an estimated </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> insects, each carrying ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons, contributing ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons in aggregate. Adding fish (~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> individuals, ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons each), birds (~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> each), and other taxa, Earth's total neural budget is approximately </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons.</span></p><p><span>A single Sun Shrimp has the same number of computational elements as the entirety of Earth's biosphere. With ~</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> granulation cells on the solar surface, the Sun can host upwards of:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>The solar ecosystem is roughly a </span><i><span>million</span></i><span> times more complex than all life on Earth combined.</span></p><h2><span>The Dyson Sphere, Climate Collapse at Scale</span></h2><p><span>Any utilitarian framework that weighs moral patient-hood by their capacity for complex experience should find this intriguing. If these computational elements map even loosely to experiential capacity, and we have no principled reason to assume they do not, then Sol's biosphere contains the possibilities of a million Earths.</span></p><p><span>To power earth and our many possible projects we have conceived of the Dyson sphere, a means of enclosing a star to harvest its radiative output. Like the amazon, we wish to take the resources but as enlightened beings we must consider the ramifications. The granulation cells that act as Sun Shrimp habitats are driven by a precise balance of radiative transport, convective instability, and magnetic field structures on the solar surface. The introduction of a massive surface harvesting energy would disrupt this by:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Alteration of the radiative boundary condition; changing the amount of radiation reflected by solar panels would disrupt the delicate balance of temperature and pressure that maintains the thermal gradients driving granulation.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Disruption of the magnetic field topology; the conductive shell of a Dyson sphere would interact with the magnetic flux at the surface, disrupting the ecological infrastructure by paving the proverbial paradise to build a parking lot</span></li></ol><p><span>We would be annihilating an ecosystem that contains a million times the complexity of our own. Indeed we should be horrified by any suggestion of modifying the solar surface for our own sick gain. We should instead work to preserve the solar surface and determine methods that allow us to keep our moral fiber intact as we move into our technological future.</span></p><p><span>So, the next time someone asks you "Have you heard of a Dyson sphere?" ask them "</span><i><span>Have you heard of a Sun Shrimp</span></i><span>?"</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/z9yAnPiS9CoruFXan/sun-shrimp-welfare#comments">Discuss</a>
